### [We urgently need to practice before we can pause](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iMKAvHSWxSoDpBoi6/we-urgently-need-to-practice-before-we-can-pause)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794006049/lexical_client_uploads/duylebxxkbhvrebqvwbl.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've noticed two clusters of thought in how people expect a global AI pause to be shaped:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Single-shot global pause</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: the global AI pause will be implemented suddenly, with little pacing precedent before it, and we will need to do a lot of research before it's implemented so that we can get it right the first time around.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Incrementally-shaped global pause</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: the global AI pause will be implemented incrementally, with lots of pacing precedent before it, and we will improve the pause setup and get more parties to join in as we go.</span></li></ol><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An incrementally-shaped global pause is both more likely and more tractable than the single-shot global pause.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This is largely because the third-party ecosystem needs practice building trust, technical competency, and general experience doing lower-scale assessments before a global pause can be competently pulled off. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also think that the concept of a single-shot global pause is an unhelpful red herring that people often implicitly use to discredit the possibility of a global pause in general.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a single-shot global pause is to happen, I think this would require substantial government buy-in, but it’s hard to build that sort of buy-in without precedent of similar things succeeding in the past. And if we just hack together a single-shot global pause without any precedent of similar things succeeding in the past, the chance that the pause falls apart due to defection, incompetence, and mistrust will be high.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The basic building block of an AI pause is trust</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The fundamental building block of an AI pause is that party A (a company or a country) can make a claim about sacrificing capability advancement for safety, and that party B can believe that claim. This is a public good, both between US AGI companies, and between countries, that is currently very scarce. Building more of this public good would generally make a pause much more likely to happen.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't just mean trust in the sense of "party B doesn't think party A is blatantly lying," but also in the broader sense that party A isn't omitting critical information or misinterpreting evidence.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Take for example the multiple cases where AGI companies have, in 2026, made statements to the effect that "we paced some of our capabilities for some safety!" These claims have been underspecified, impossible to verify from the outside, and have often been silently disbelieved by much of the industry. This air of distrust has made it much harder for companies to engage in tit-for-tat strategies where companies can become safer/slower as other companies become safer/slower.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Currently, a lot of people at the frontier AGI companies would prefer a state where AI progress is slower across the entire industry, but they have no way of trusting each other to actually uphold their commitments. We need to enter a different state where, instead of general distrust, parties can sacrifice capabilities for safety in a way that other parties actually believe.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can practice building trust before the global pause</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even small engagements between third parties and AGI companies can meaningfully improve trust between companies. Imagine if the companies that made claims about sacrificing capabilities for safety in 2026 actually had third parties verify those claims to each other. This would have made those sacrifices much more impactful towards incentivizing other companies to follow suit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You might say "But verifying those claims in 2026 would have been very difficult and would have cost too many resources of the third parties and the AGI companies! We don't have the science or hardware to verify these claims now!" I don't believe this at all. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's assume that companies aren’t actively deceiving third-party evaluators by e.g. selectively surfacing logs, coaching employees to present a false story, or secretly running compute on undisclosed systems. It seems very easy to verify a claim to the effect that "Our company stopped a specific class of training run during a specific time interval." You just need a handful of people from a third party to go in, conduct some interviews, inspect some logs, and cross-check their findings. Assuming good </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-10-14-our-approach-to-risk-assessment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">process transparency</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the public and other AGI companies would very likely believe the claims verified by the third party. Also, process transparency can protect both against minor deception and inadequate access, as the third party can directly tell the public that they weren’t able to arrive at proper conclusions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The fact that more such engagements didn’t happen in 2026 seems like a major missed opportunity to me.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can improve procedures and technical competency with practice</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If third parties get lots of experience assessing safety-relevant claims, then they can build a knowledge base over time that makes the pause more robust. There are likely a number of technical and organizational pitfalls about verifying pauses that are hard to avoid without prior experience doing lower-stakes projects of a similar kind. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Contrast this with a vision where most of the setup of the pause is figured out without practicing subsets of it first. Maybe lots of people write lots of memos and papers about how to make the pause good. Without grounding in reality, companies and countries will be hard to convince that the pause will actually succeed and that other parties will uphold their commitments.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pacing is not a scarce resource</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’ve heard many people express the sentiment that we need to not pause/pace right now and save our pause/pacing activities for later when they’re more useful. This is based on a model where pacing right now makes it less likely that we will be able to pace in the future. I think this model is exactly backwards for the reasons I’ve laid out, like establishing technical competency, trust, and relationships necessary for successful pausing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, we should view pacing right now as enabling more involved pacing activities (including a pause) later.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a real worry that, if we spend our political capital now and cause AGI companies to slow down for no good reason, then this might lead to pauses being seen as useless, and therefore less likely to happen, going forward. I think we should keep this in mind, but I don't expect it to outweigh the benefit of enabling better pausing later. Part of why I think the downside is relatively small is that there appears to be no such rhetoric surrounding temporary slowdowns AGI companies have announced in 2026 (although again, few people took those seriously due to mistrust, so this provides limited evidence). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More importantly, the risk from backlash is mostly from slowdowns that seem arbitrary and unproductive, but I expect that we're at a point where companies could use breathing room to conduct large projects to make their systems safer, and third parties could help verify that companies are actually investing in safety during slowdowns.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concrete recommendations for third-party assessments</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concretely, the following kinds of third-party assessments seem most valuable for setting up the right conditions for a global pause:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Measuring things relevant to a pause: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assessments focused on compute allocation and RSI-relevant metrics (e.g. algorithmic efficiency, uplift measures, spend on AI agents, analysis of success rates on internal tasks) seem most relevant for creating the right kind of technical competency and relationships.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Commitment-focused: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ideally, third parties would verify a claim that a company made about a commitment they intend to uphold.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">High-access: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assessors would ideally get however much access is given to employees that track similar issues.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cross-company: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a third-party assessment simultaneously involves multiple AGI companies, this setup is more similar to the kind of setup we might want during a global pause.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">International: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Getting practice verifying claims between the US and China seems extremely valuable, although it might be out of scope for current third-party orgs without explicit endorsement from the US government. US-UK or US-EU collaborations could provide some lower-stakes practice.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Going from US-only pause to global pause will likely require hardware</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’d guess that we don’t need any special hardware expertise to verify a US-only pause, we just need a few dozen experienced FTEs at a handful of third-party organizations that have done high-access assessments with AGI companies. Plausibly even a single third-party org with enough access and around a dozen FTEs would have enough capacity to verify a US-only pause as long as the AGI company employees were cooperative.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a global pause, I’d guess that we will need substantial investment into hardware research, because trust and understanding are much lower between the US and China than between companies within the US. If tomorrow, the US government entrusted some competent third party to fly a dozen of their staff to China and verify that a Chinese company paused their training runs, I think that the combination of language barriers, cultural barriers, lack of organizational context, lack of existing relationships, time zone differences, strained international relations, and general increased latency would make it much harder to verify things than in a US-only context.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Incrementally-shaped” does not mean slow</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s plausible that AI R&amp;D will be fully automated by the end of 2027. As a result we might have under a year to make a successful pause happen.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think we need to incrementally shape a pause as soon as possible, and we need to very quickly increase the ambition of third-party engagements if we want the technical competency and trust in third parties to reach the appropriate level before it’s too late.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An example timeline of what it might look like to incrementally shape a global pause given ASI in mid-2028 by default:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are here: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a third party has reported important RSI-relevant metrics from within an AGI company in a way that other companies and the public believe.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q1 2027: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a third party publishes a compute allocation pilot with multiple AGI companies participating and employee-level access, publicly reporting the percentage of compute they spend on capabilities and safety over the last year.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q2 2027: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a company stops a frontier training run for three weeks because of a warning shot where a model tried to majorly sabotage the company. A third party verifies that the training run and other similarly-sized training runs have actually been stopped. Other companies and the public believe this.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q3 2027: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a third party is facilitating a setup where every frontier AGI company in the US regularly (e.g. every two weeks) reports the most important RSI-relevant metrics.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q4 2027: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">all frontier AGI companies in the US agree to (or are forced to) enter a pause on further capabilities progress or strict compute cap regime (e.g. where they can spend only 10% of compute on capabilities). Their adherence is verified by a third party (or a government body that substantially depends on people hired/contracted from a third party) with more than a year of experience verifying similar claims.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q1 2028: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the rest of the world joins in on the pause, and the third-party manpower increases substantially to cover the additional labor required to verify commitments between countries. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t have detailed takes on how exactly hardware verification scales up in 2027 in the scenario to meet the moment in 2028 to verify a global pause (because I don’t know much about hardware verification). Presumably, a number of smaller pilots happen in 2027 with multiple AGI companies participating, which leads to lessons learned for verifying a global pause.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Third parties need to very quickly scale up the ambition of the collaborations they are doing with AGI companies. If they fall notably behind the rough timeline I laid out (as measured by the scale of third-party engagements done by AGI companies), I would be pretty pessimistic about a pause being doable without substantial government buy-in, and even if it did happen I would be pessimistic about it succeeding.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iMKAvHSWxSoDpBoi6/we-urgently-need-to-practice-before-we-can-pause#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Higher salaries in AI safety](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HtMvB7XEFThvDLLzT/higher-salaries-in-ai-safety)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The salaries are too damn low</span></h3><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/HtMvB7XEFThvDLLzT/hfkfinijlauecgn8edoo" /><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety has a salary overhang. A bunch of people are paid way below their market rate. (Also true of EA, but I’ll lump them under “AI safety” for this piece).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This has been getting a bit better, very recently — I hear about eg Redwood Research technical talent making ~$800k/y in salary. But when you compare this to the $10-100m/y comp packages for talent in labs, it still seems kind of low. And CG’s money cannon has fired very few shots to date — maybe ~15 orgs hit, see </span><a href="http://trace.manifund.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Trace</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — so the raises are still very unequally distributed.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s not just rank-and-file employees, by the way. Even the leadership is grossly underpaid. The numbers I hear range from $150k - $600k/y, for execs at top orgs like METR, MATS, </span><a href="http://far.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FAR.AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Constellation. Like, c’mon, I made $250k/y total comp as a undistinguished new grad software engineer in 2016 Google. For the leaders of preeminent safety orgs, surely we can do better than that.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I were a conspiracy theorist, I might say that “accepting lower salaries” is a </span><a href="https://tvtropes.org/pmwiki/pmwiki.php/Main/NoHeroDiscount"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">psyop from funders</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Low salaries mean that employees and execs remain beholden to funders, rather than having the independence to work on what makes their heart sing. For me, hitting </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIRE_movement"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FIRE</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> early in my tech career allowed me to quit my job and work on side projects and startups. To this day, I feel more independent from the “AI safety hivemind” because my paycheck doesn’t depend on it.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Paying low salaries is also penny-wise, pound-foolish from the perspective of funders &amp; orgs. It means that great talent ends up choosing not to work in safety roles. A lot of the safety brain drain is because the labs are bidding much higher.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, funder &lt;&gt; org &lt;&gt; employee values are much more aligned within AI safety than in the wider world. Much of what these employees might buy with extra money are things that would be good by the lights of the funder &amp; the org — eg donating to charities or other important causes. (Orgs could offer large donation matches in lieu of cash to enforce this, though I think donation matches have their own problems.)</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In practice, one big problem might be that the employees themselves don’t really know what they’re worth. My kingdom for a </span><a href="http://levels.fyi/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">levels.fyi</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of AI safety.</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety people like to point out that frontier lab employees currently have some leverage over their leaders, and should use this leverage to advocate for good policies (eg pace), before they’re all automated away by AI. I’d point out that AI safety employees themselves are in similarly precarious positions. How long before automated AI safety researchers?</span></li><li value="10"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One </span><a href="https://manifund.substack.com/p/sixteen-schemes-for-ai-safety"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scheme</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I’m fond of is “lab employee union (but, good)”. Similarly, it might be interesting to have an “AI safety employee union (but, good)”.</span></li></ol><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">… or are they fine, actually?</span></h3><ol start="11"><li value="11"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One pushback against higher salaries is they’re already pretty high, for the reference class of nonprofit work. Like $100k/y might be enough to cover living costs and help individuals build up some savings, even in high cost of living locations like SF/DC/NYC/London. But, idk, I want safety people to feel safe, and thrive, and have babies.</span></li><li value="12"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some argue that offering lower salaries leads to more mission-oriented employees. I think there’s some truth to this, but also some amount of cope. And this kind of reasoning can let an org undercount the opportunity cost of its people, causing internal &amp; ecosystem-wide misallocation of talent. So put your money where your mouth is!</span></li><li value="13"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe it looks bad somehow, if nonprofit employees &amp; execs are making $1m+/y? To that, I’d say: a) </span><a href="https://manifund.substack.com/p/funding-for-profits-with-charitable"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">stop creating nonprofits</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, b) inform your critics about how much the labs are pay, and c) maybe just don’t worry so much about looking bad.</span></li><li value="14"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">IMO the strongest pushback for safety executives, specifically, is that they already have a large amount of steering power, from their ability to direct the org budget &amp; hire people. (This is partly why I’ve never taken a salary at Manifund. And I do appreciate that Sam Altman roughly does the same.)</span></li></ol><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gigabrained compensation</span></h3><ol start="15"><li value="15"><a href="https://manifund.substack.com/p/what-happened-to-impact-markets"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Impact equity</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> would solve many of these salary considerations. (Apologies to the choir for the preaching.) Equity allocation is what allowed the startup ecosystem flourish — and now, the frontier lab ecosystem too. The “do good” space is immensely </span><a href="https://manifund.substack.com/p/dont-default-to-nonprofit"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hobbled by nonprofit charity norms</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="16"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BTW, one reason </span><a href="https://manifund.substack.com/p/regranting-in-2026-now-more-than"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we love regranting</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is that it corrects for some of the problems introduced by low nonprofit salaries. Great AI safety people get the funds and the mandate to go around “angel investing” into new good opportunities.</span></li><li value="17"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond higher salaries, I’m tentatively in favor of a UBI or pension for AI safety people. Maybe: $50k/y paid out to forever, to anyone who meets certain objective thresholds, eg 6+ months of fulltime safety employment or funding. I’d hope for a renaissance where individuals feel empowered to move, quit, fund weird things, explore and start little projects.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This wouldn’t be that expensive! I’d guess 10k people might qualify currently, so this would cost $500M/y — peanuts, compared to Anthropic wealth. Obviously there’s room to do design this better, but I think UBI should be one baseline for “how to distribute Anthropic wealth”. (I don’t know that current funding structures even beat this baseline.)</span></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HtMvB7XEFThvDLLzT/higher-salaries-in-ai-safety#comments">Discuss</a>
