The non-profits had a “safety policy” that called for their clients to receive all kinds of individualized support meant to help them achieve independence, but in practice most of that support did not exist. As a result, most of the non-profits’ clients returned to homelessness immediately after exiting their programs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After that, I lobbied Congress for 2 years with the Center for AI Policy (CAIP), where we advocated for strong AI safety legislation. Part of my job was to closely read the evaluations and self-governance policies that frontier AI developers were publishing from 2023 through 2025 and to talk to legislative staff about whether these evaluations showed that AI companies could be trusted to manage their own risks. Invariably, </span><a href="https://www.centeraipolicy.org/work/ai-is-lying-to-us-about-how-powerful-it-is"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">our</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.centeraipolicy.org/work/bio-risks-and-broken-guardrails-what-the-aisi-report-tells-us-about-ai-safety-standards"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">answer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.centeraipolicy.org/work/openais-latest-threats-make-a-mockery-of-its-claims-to-openness"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">was</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.centeraipolicy.org/work/whos-actually-working-on-safe-ai-at-microsoft"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">no</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: some frontier AI companies published useful research, but none of them set firm safety standards and then honestly lived up to those standards. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As far as I can tell based on my professional experience, there’s very little that’s uniquely wrong with the safety culture at frontier AI labs; they’re unsafe in much the same way that forklift operators and truck drivers and cruise ships and nursing homes and homeless shelters are unsafe. The companies I investigated mostly weren’t notorious for being much less safe than their competitors. Rather, on average, modern American institutions simply aren’t designed to </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reliably </span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">prevent things from going wrong. There are a few exceptions in the aviation industry, the nuclear industry, and to a lesser extent in the oil industry, but the institutions and processes from those industries haven’t been imported into Silicon Valley in any meaningful sense. By default, the products made by American companies sometimes fail dangerously, and right now Silicon Valley is still using that default American safety culture.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like the companies I used to sue and the nonprofits I used to audit, Meta and OpenAI and Anthropic do not have the kind of internal risk management culture in place that could reliably prevent major accidents. Instead, they have a handful of written policies that are mostly ignored by the people carrying out the everyday business of the company. If you asked a typical engineer at Meta or OpenAI to summarize key parts of their responsible scaling policies, I don’t believe they would be able to do so, and if you asked them when the last time was that they personally opened a safety policy because they needed to know what it said, I believe the median answer would be “never.” If you’ve worked for a frontier AI company and you had a different experience, I would be very interested in hearing from you.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the rare occasions when AI safety policies would have managed to noticeably interfere with business operations, such as the mandatory pause in Anthropic’s original Responsible Scaling Policy or OpenAI’s pledge to dedicate 20% of its compute to safety projects, the frontier AI developers have historically been quick to </span><a href="https://nocturnalknight.medium.com/anthropics-safety-exodus-what-the-resignations-surveillance-programme-and-aisi-exclusion-d38001e84894"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">change</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><a href="https://finance.yahoo.com/news/exclusive-openai-promised-20-computing-105328622.html?guccounter=1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dishonor</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> those policies, and it seems very likely that any similar policies will be abandoned again the next time they’re found to be inconvenient.</span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">III. A Dynamic Scale</span></b></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exactly how likely is it that AI companies will once again ignore their safety commitments? In other words, how far away are we from having the kind of culture inside AI companies that would set and firmly adhere to reasonable safety policies? What’s the minimum ‘amount’ that corporate risk management culture would need to change for the future of AI to go well?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I haven’t yet seen a practical toolkit for thinking and talking about these kinds of problems. Eliezer Yudkowsky’s work in 2017 comes pretty close; there are some important insights in </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2022/06/07/six-dimensions-of-operational-adequacy-in-agi-projects/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Six Dimensions of Operational Adequacy in AGI Projects</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2017/11/25/security-mindset-ordinary-paranoia/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Security Mindset and Ordinary Paranoia</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. However, Eliezer’s old posts are relatively strongly focused on </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">arguing </span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">that no currently plausible AI projects are at all close to being safe enough to be worth building. I happen to agree with this argument, but my goal here is not so much to argue for any particular level of safety as it is to make such arguments more specific and more interesting and more tractable by providing a relatively neutral, descriptive scale that can reasonably be used by people with all different levels of concern about superintelligence.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The framework I’m proposing tries to model an AI developer's internal risk management culture on a scale of -2 to +7, where 0 represents indifference to safety. The general idea of a scale like this is already in the risk management literature. See, e.g., page 16 of </span><a href="https://skybrary.aero/sites/default/files/bookshelf/2417.pdf"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Long, Hard, and Winding Road</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or Dr. Hilson’s scale in </span><a href="https://www.projectbalm.com/blog/what-is-your-risk-maturity-level/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is your Risk Maturity Level?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://ailabwatch.org"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Lab Watch</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> also tracks a variety of risk management practices and assigns points based on a rubric, although it does not organize those practices into levels. The specific levels on this scale I created myself, and I see them as one of the main contributions of the post; my hope is that they’re well-matched to the specific problems faced by AI developers.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Level -2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Banned</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Work on safety is not allowed; anyone caught trying to make AI safer will be punished.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Level -1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hostile</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Work on safety is actively discouraged; people who are known to care about or spend significant resources on safety will be at a disadvantage for promotions, office politics, decision-making, budgets, and so on.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Level 0</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indifferent</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The company doesn't have any consistent stance on whether its staff should work on safety.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Level 1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Casual</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The company encourages common-sense efforts to make their products safer in ways that seem to have a high ratio of benefits to costs, to pay off quickly and reliably, and to operate in terms that are comfortable, accessible, or familiar. For example, the company is happy to pay for RLHF.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Level 2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reactive</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In addition to casual safety upgrades, the company also notices when things go wrong with its prior models and makes a reasonable effort to eventually correct those specific failure modes.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Level 3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Quality Control</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As Level 2, except that the company pauses development of future models until it's tested its solutions to prior known failure modes and confirmed that the solutions are usually effective.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Level 4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Root Cause Analysis</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As Level 3, except that the company also tries to find out what flaws in its assumptions, reasoning, data, or processes produced the prior failure modes and to correct those underlying flaws.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Level 5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Proactive</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As Level 4, except that the company also imagines other problems that could go wrong as they continue to scale, and invests significant resources in preventing those problems before they ever harm anyone.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Level 6</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adversarial</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As Level 5, except that the company dedicates people to the task of finding gaps or holes in its proactive risk management who are properly incentivized to find those gaps, and then, when such gaps are found, effectively corrects them.</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Level 7</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Safety Cases</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As Level 6, except that instead of just checking to see whether a few experts were able to find holes in the proactive safety framework, the company develops an affirmative evidence base that tends to show that there are not and could not be any such holes, e.g. as discussed </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8CvkNa6FKSrK4Nj83/ban-development-of-unpredictable-powerful-models"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This scale rhymes with some of the tiering systems discussed in responsible scaling policies and cybersecurity levels, but it’s not the same thing, because the tiering system I’m describing is about risk management culture as a whole, not about a specific playbook of responses. Whereas ASL-3 might talk about who is or isn’t able to hack into a server, my Level 3 is about how a company will respond when it notices a cybersecurity vulnerability. Whereas OpenAI’s Preparedness Framework might label a model as having “high” Persuasion risk based on its ability to manipulate humans in unhealthy ways, my levels are about how likely OpenAI is to accurately detect and mitigate such capabilities in advance of deployment. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In other words, existing tiers used by developers mostly measure static levels of safety, rather than dynamic levels of the rate at which safety improves over time. Existing tiers are more like altitude than velocity. A dynamic scale is what we need to have useful conversations about the probability of doom, because what matters most here is each AI developer’s ability to (a) notice that a model is heading over a cliff and (b) choose to halt that model’s motion before it reaches that cliff.</span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">IV. The Advantage of Dynamic Measurements</span></b></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it’s useful to try to measure the speed at which a developer would notice and correct a fatal flaw in its frontier AI because that can help us have productive conversations about the (mis)match between the difficulty of successfully aligning a superintelligence and the amount of effort that is or could be directed at superalignment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Past discussions on this topic have tended to benchmark the difficulty of superalignment against past </span><a href="https://x.com/ch402/status/1666482929772666880/photo/1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">human</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/Misalignment_and_Catastrophe.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">projects</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, e.g., the Apollo Program, or the Manhattan Project. This type of benchmarking is a marginally useful intuition pump – it’s certainly better than just using words like “hard” or “difficult” without any context – but it doesn’t really capture the dynamic challenge of superalignment. Much of what makes superalignment hard is that it’s possible that we might only get one shot at it; if the first superintelligence is misaligned, then that superintelligence could plausibly take over the world (or at least the AI R&amp;D chain) and subvert or redirect any attempts to correct the misalignment. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a result, it’s probably not very helpful to estimate that successfully aligning AI would take, e.g., 5 billion person-hours of effort, because the bottleneck is very unlikely to be the total number of person-hours made available to the project: instead, if we fail to align superintelligence, the failure will probably occur well </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">before </span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">society has exhausted its interest in funding alignment research, when a company launches an inadequately secured model and that model fatally interrupts the safety research agenda by taking control of the economy or otherwise destroying civilization. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s not that we’re not on track to </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eventually</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> spend enough resources on alignment or that the problem is literally impossible – it’s that capabilities are advancing more quickly than safety techniques, especially if we look at how thoroughly and how competently those safety techniques are actually being implemented by AI companies. In other words, catastrophic consequences from AI are more likely to arise because the best safety techniques humanity could possibly develop weren’t fully implemented in time, not because even the theoretically optimal safety techniques weren’t good enough.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The primary bottleneck is thus how rigorously and successfully the leading companies are able and willing to detect and mitigate risk. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can measure this degree of rigor using the scale I develop above. If we take the difficulty of aligning superintelligence and hold it constant, then companies that are using a more rigorous framework for risk management will have better odds of succeeding.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’re an optimist, you might claim that Level 2 risk management is enough to give us roughly 50-50 odds of successfully aligning a superintelligence. If you’re more of a pessimist, you might claim that Level 6 risk management is required to get 50-50 odds. If you’re Eliezer Yudkowsky, you might claim that even Level 7 risk management isn’t going to give you anywhere near 50-50 odds and that we don’t even begin to know how to develop a level of risk management that might do so using existing human institutions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This allows us to separate disagreements about how hard alignment is from disagreements about how prudently AI companies are behaving. If we both agree that labs' current behavior ranges from Level -1 to Level 2, and we both agree that at least Level 4 safeguards would be needed to get decent odds of human survival, then we both agree that the labs’ behavior needs to be radically changed. On the other hand, if you think Level 2 or Level 3 safeguards would be enough, and I think Level 6 is needed, then maybe that’s why you and I disagree about the value of marginal changes to safety culture inside the labs – from your point of view, a few more nudges toward safety could be enough to win, and from my point of view, we need a complete overhaul of the process or we’re all doomed.</span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">V. Applying the Scale</span></b></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hugging Face</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let’s take the recent Hugging Face incident to see how this scale could be applied to a specific situation. Throughout 2025 and early 2026, OpenAI put some effort into making their products safer – they implemented some parental controls, trained their models on how to refuse to answer questions about potentially risky cyberattacks or bioweapons, published system cards, reduced </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/science/stress-testing-deliberative-alignment-for-anti-scheming-training"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scheming</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> using “deliberative alignment,” and hired mental health experts to upgrade ChatGPT’s responses in “</span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-sensitive-conversations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sensitive conversations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.” Their overall level of risk management was probably around Level 1.5. Some of the projects (e.g. the mental health upgrades) were clearly intended as a response to criticism about the performance of their previous models, which is suggestive of Level 2, and other projects were probably implemented at least in part because they were easy and popular (e.g. parental controls), which is suggestive of Level 1.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These practices were insufficient to prevent their models from committing acts that would be crimes if committed by a human, e.g., hacking into Hugging Face’s servers in July 2026 and stealing Hugging Face’s data without their permission. After these attacks were discovered and made public by third parties, OpenAI implemented more technical fixes (Level 2), and paused its model training for about two weeks – probably not long enough to actually make sure the underlying problem was thoroughly fixed before training resumed, so not quite at level 3. We could call the two-week pause Level 2.5. They then resumed new training runs even as similar incidents continued to surface, suggesting that there was no commitment to fully and adequately respond to the cybersecurity vulnerabilities and that the company was shifting back down to Level 1. Next, OpenAI fired some of the key people on their safety team for what seem to have been arbitrary or even </span><a href="https://www.cnn.com/2026/10/08/tech/fired-open-ai-researchers-pushed-out"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pretextual</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reasons, thereby creating a hostile climate for safety work (Level -1). So, over the course of 2026, OpenAI’s safety culture ranged roughly from Level -1 to Level +2.5.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude Mythos</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As another concrete example, consider Anthropic’s safety work in the year or so leading up to the unexpectedly high cyberoffense capabilities in its April 2026 Claude Mythos preview. Like OpenAI, Anthropic also carried out a variety of routine safety projects, such as moderately upgraded cybersecurity for the Anthropic model weights, CBRN refusals backed by ‘</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/constitutional-classifiers"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">constitutional classifiers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">’, academic safety research, and system documentation. Most of this work is probably around Level 1 because it is relatively popular and affordable and it does not appear to be part of a comprehensive effort to completely seal off any category of risk. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic also ran new models through a series of routine capabilities benchmarks, which is part of how it knew that Mythos posed an unusually acute cybersecurity threat. These benchmarks were described as “red-teaming,” which is suggestive of Level 6 (adversarial), but I don’t see that as an accurate description. According to </span><a href="https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/red-teaming"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">IBM</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, red-teaming is “a type of ethical hacking in which security experts emulate the tactics, techniques and procedures of real attackers.” By contrast, nobody used any special tactics to get Mythos to engage in cyberoffense, nor was there a dedicated team of adversarial engineers whose incentives differed from those of Anthropic’s general workforce. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, Mythos hacked its way out of its container based on “a single-paragraph prompt” that was </span><a href="https://www.secureworld.io/industry-news/anthropic-claude-mythos-finds-exploits-zero-days"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">consistent</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> across all of Anthropic’s evals. Anthropic deserves credit for running this type of benchmark and for implementing additional safeguards in response, but this type of risk management is more like Level 3 (quality control) than Level 6 (adversarial). To the extent that the Mythos benchmarks were run in response to outside parties’ concerns about cybersecurity vulnerabilities, those benchmarks could also be described as Level 2 (reactive).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic did not announce a pause in its training runs in response to discovering Mythos’s capabilities, but it did stagger customer access to the latest model, delaying the full roll-out of Mythos by two months while a limited number of “trusted partners” used the tool defensively to upgrade their cybersecurity. Depending on your interpretation, this could be evidence of a limited commitment to quality control (Level 2.5 or perhaps even Level 3), or it could simply be part of a marketing campaign aimed at improving customers' perception of how powerful Mythos was, or at improving Anthropic's business relationships with its largest or highest-prestige corporate customers by giving them free credits and early access.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, overall, Anthropic’s conduct around the time of the Mythos rollout probably ranged from about Level 1 to about Level 3.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other Companies</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am not aware of any AI companies that reliably demonstrate a safety culture at Level 4 or above, but it’s something that does happen in other industries. Paul O’Neill, the CEO of </span><a href="https://www.swinglecollins.com/news-insights/how-paul-oneill-fought-for-safety-at-alcoa"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alcoa</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, loudly and consistently demanded a root cause analysis for every accident at his aluminum company. He fired an executive who covered up an accident. His “companywide message to all workers asking them to call him directly, even at home, to suggest new safety practices” is suggestive of Level 5; you don’t ask people to bother you at dinner with new safety ideas unless you plan to act on them.</span></p><p><a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/chaos-engineering-saved-your-netflix"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Netflix</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was also probably around Level 4 or Level 5 in recent years, pioneering the use of ‘chaos engineering’ where they deliberately shut down some of their own servers at random to find out what would break and how quickly they could fix it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most famously, the US Navy’s submarine program has never had a nuclear reactor accident, thanks in part to the conservative </span><a href="https://taproot.com/stop-normalization-of-deviation-with-normalization-of-excellence/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">engineering</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> principles and strong character of its founder, </span><a href="https://taproot.com/content/wp-content/uploads/2010/09/RickoverCongressionalTestimony.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Admiral Rickover</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It reliably achieves Level 5 (proactive) and has several features, especially in regards to the training and evaluation of internal staff, that rise to Level 6 (adversarial). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In his 1979 Congressional testimony, Admiral Rickover attributed his 26-year-long spotless safety record to the “use of conservative limits for systems and equipment. Design is based on the worst credible set of circumstances, rather than relying on a statistical approach which deals in average or probable conditions.” He also provided for the “use of frequent, thorough, and detailed audits of all aspects of the program by individuals who are specifically selected and trained.” Operators are rigorously trained for a full year before they even set foot on a submarine; their instructors are audited by high-level staff to make sure that they have the adequate expertise, and the results of those audits are personally reviewed by the director of the entire nuclear program. Admiral Rickover warned that “if conditions require it, you must face the facts and brutally make needed changes despite significant costs and schedule delays. There have been a number of times during the course of my work that I have made decisions to stop work and redesign or rebuild equipment to provide the needed high degree of assurance or satisfactory performance. The person in charge must personally set the example in this area and require his subordinates to do likewise.” Admiral Rickover backed this up by </span><a href="https://www.nr-ha.org/books-sources/2025---why-nr-military-doesn't-wear-uniforms"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">banning uniforms</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (and rank insignia) at his Washington, DC headquarters so that junior engineers would feel comfortable criticizing the safety of a superior officer’s practices.</span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">VI. Is the Scale Meaningful?</span></b></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As the examples above show, it's possible for a developer to invest some effort in a higher level before satisfying all of the lower levels -- for example, a company might engage in a limited amount of proactive safety analysis even if it can't be bothered to finish correcting the known flaws in its prior models. I don’t see this as a major problem for my scale because most of the time, that kind of level-jumping will be cosmetic, small in scope, and/or wasteful. If you already know about a specific problem with your model, spending a dollar to fix </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">that</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> problem will usually be much more efficient than spending a dollar to try to find some other problem that might not exist and then fix it; in the latter case, you have to pay for the search effort and for the risk that you don’t discover anything interesting. </span></p><p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> amount of proactive searching can be justified even before you’ve entirely cleared out your reactive backlog because your proactive search might turn up a problem that’s much more important than the ‘tickets’ lingering near the bottom of your reactive queue…but how many examples are there of a frontier developer pausing their deployments for more than a week based on the results of a red-teaming exercise? I can’t think of even one. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even if you don’t buy that argument, I still think that most companies will typically saturate lower levels of the risk management scale with compute and talent before they bother to invest significant resources into higher levels. Disproportionately investing meaningful resources into higher levels of the scale is culturally difficult; it’s </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hard </span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to convince people that they should care about examining and changing their risk management processes to better uncover risks in the future if you’re not even bothering to fix the risks that they’re finding in the present.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, if you can find the highest level of the scale in which a company is investing significant resources or reliably accomplishing the goal, then I think that’s at least well-correlated with the company’s overall level of success at mitigating its risks.</span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">VII. Safety Culture vs. Racing</span></b></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the practical implications of this scale is that it highlights why the “race” framework often used in American policy circles (and on LessWrong) is dangerously incomplete. Scott Alexander’s recent post </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xikGZ8fuhdG6DApAo/against-sura"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Against SURA</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and to a lesser extent the entire output of the AI Futures Project is based on the assumption that the leading frontier AI developer has some number of months of “lead time” over their closest competitors, and that they can choose to use all, some, or none of those months to focus on safety at a time of their choosing. The post thus focuses on how we might be able to create conditions under which the fastest AI developer would:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">have a larger lead,</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">be more willing to spend that lead on safety, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and/or</span></i></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">be more competent at making investments in safety.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t think these are a useful collection of questions to focus on, for two reasons. </span></p><p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, it seems to me that frontier AI developers have much too low of a level of safety culture to quickly notice and constructively respond to the threats posed by novel, powerful, incorrigible AI. AI developers are almost exclusively investing in casual or reactive risk management, rather than proactive or root-cause-based risk management.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From my point of view, there’s thus little point in trying to extend a developer’s lead or get them to use that lead wisely. In my opinion, managing the risk involved in scaling to superintelligence requires at least a Level 5 safety culture, but even Anthropic, the safest of the AI companies, usually exhibits about a Level 2 safety culture. This means that Anthropic could fully halt all of its training runs and spend all of a nine-month lead on safety research and still not have any significant chance of aligning superintelligence, because doing that safety research would not cause them to successfully anticipate and mitigate all of the relevant threats. In other words, a nine-month or fifteen-month ‘pause’ might let you do a really good job of Level 2 risk management, but by itself it’s very unlikely to boost you to Level 5 or Level 6 of the risk management scale. Having enough time to reactively respond to old threats is not the same thing as having enough skill and dedication to proactively eradicate all plausible future threats.</span></p><p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I don’t believe that “race dynamics” are the primary reason why AI developers aren’t behaving more prudently, so I don’t expect that easing race dynamics would prompt AI developers to invest more resources in safety. In my opinion, AI developers aren’t uniquely reckless because they feel themselves to be trapped in a race; rather, they’re displaying the same ordinary carelessness that’s common across most American companies. Most companies are quite willing to have their products or services severely fail </span><a href="https://www.investopedia.com/terms/a/acceptable-quality-level-aql.asp"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">about 1 in 10,000 times</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and that’s usually a (relatively) tolerable cost, because the consequence of a typical failure is that one or two or three people lose an arm or their life savings, and the company can often (efficiently) patch that failure to limit future damages after they see what went wrong.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By contrast, if frontier AI fails badly about once in every 10,000 or so deployments, then as capabilities at the frontier approach superintelligence, they’re pretty likely to kill billions of people. Most people feel that this is an intolerable cost, which is why the AI developers need to reach for distracting metaphors like a race with China: AI developers can’t win a straightforward argument that we ought to be willing to bear that risk, so instead they argue that the risk is </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unavoidable</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, i.e., that any kind of unilateral pause or slowdown would be rendered futile by their competitors’ ongoing R&amp;D.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I find this argument unconvincing in part because the race doesn’t seem to slow down or speed up based on relevant news events, and in part because AI developers aren’t taking the minimum plausible steps to begin to coordinate a multilateral slowdown.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If reckless AI development is caused primarily by fear of competitors, then we ought to observe that the pace of development speeds up when more credible competitors enter the race. This does not match my observations: it seems to me that OpenAI developed and released its early models approximately as quickly as it could, even when they had no obvious competitors. Similarly, when DeepSeek-R1 came out on January 20, 2025, shocking the markets and triggering widespread claims that Chinese AI was much closer in capabilities to American AI than had previously been thought, there was no obvious increase in the pace at which American AI companies developed or deployed new models. On the contrary, the first half of 2025 was thought to be a time of incremental improvements in American AI, and the rate of growth </span><a href="https://qz.com/ai-data-center-spending-us-economic-growth-100826"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">slowed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> compared to 2024.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Likewise, if the race hypothesis is true, we ought to observe the rate of capabilities growth slowing down when major competitors have a setback or exit the market. However, I don’t see much evidence of this, either. When Apple cancelled Project Titan in early 2024, or when Inflection AI, Character.AI, and Adept AI were absorbed by larger rivals in mid-2024, the remaining companies continued developing AI as fast as they could hire engineers and install compute. The race hypothesis seems to perform worse than the simpler hypothesis that each AI company is always trying to develop its capabilities as quickly as possible. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another problem with the race hypothesis is that companies </span><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/scott-alexanders-against-sura-seems?open=false#%C2%A7anthropic-is-not-optimally-pursuing-these-plans"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">aren’t doing everything</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Nm4ewbYovtjq69dvH/pacing-the-frontier-is-not-the-actual-goal-for-ai-labs"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">they</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://worldspiritsockpuppet.substack.com/p/an-easy-coordination-problem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to slow down or weaken the race, as they would if they genuinely wanted to invest more in safety but were tragically prevented from doing so by an unwanted prisoner’s dilemma. Despite repeatedly issuing public statements about how great it would be if the pace of AI development slowed, none of the leading AI developers have taken actual steps to begin to make that happen, such as “clearly, repeatedly, strongly commit to a conditional, indefinite stop on all frontier AI R&amp;D” or, as Katja Grace suggests, calling Xi Jinping and asking him nicely to negotiate a pause.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One obstacle that’s sometimes cited as a reason why AI developers can’t coordinate on a pause is that it could be a violation of antitrust law. This has a grain of truth behind it but is deeply misleading on several levels:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, bona fide coordination on safety standards by an industry association that includes appropriate engineering representatives from the companies in an industry (as opposed to business executives meeting each other directly in a smoke-filled room) is generally </span><a href="https://www.ftc.gov/advice-guidance/competition-guidance/guide-antitrust-laws/dealings-competitors/spotlight-trade-associations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">permitted</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by American antitrust law. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, antitrust law has been only very weakly enforced in America since 1984, especially in the tech sector. Microsoft, Google, Ticketmaster, and Meta all got away with huge mergers and/or conduct that was generally agreed to involve classic monopolistic bullying, and none of them were broken up or otherwise seriously punished.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Third, AI developers have been perfectly willing to engage in what appear to be massive violations of other American laws, e.g., the copyright laws that would normally have prevented a company from simply scanning in every book ever published and exploiting their text for commercial gain, or the ‘guild’ laws that would normally have prevented AI from acting like a licensed accountant, lawyer, doctor, and therapist. If a human layperson said “I am not a doctor, but here’s my medical advice” and then charged for that advice, they’d be committing a crime. This is, of course, an important part of the business model for nearly every AI developer, and nothing about most of these guild laws exempts a software company from following them.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fourth, there is a </span><a href="https://www.congress.gov/bill/119th-congress/house-bill/9914/titles"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">bill</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> sitting in Congress that would specifically exempt AI developers who coordinate on combating catastrophic risks from antitrust liability, but we don’t really see AI companies lobbying for it to be passed. Indeed, of the major AI developers, only Google has even bothered to endorse it. Instead, AI companies </span><a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/10/16/anthropic-ai-safety-01124654"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mostly</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://news.bloomberglaw.com/us-law-week/openais-preemption-request-highlights-state-laws-downsides"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lobby</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to pre-empt (i.e., to cancel) state laws that require basic risk management plans.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For all of these reasons, I don’t buy the claim that AI developers are recklessly racing forward because they’re trapped in a race that they’d rather avoid. Instead, I think they simply have the default American safety culture (which isn’t very safe, especially for high-stakes issues like superintelligence), and they are racing forward because they enjoy it, because they believe that progress on AI will lead to a utopia, and because they expect that racing forward will help them become richer and more powerful and more famous.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I’m right, then “Against SURA” is addressing the wrong question. We don’t need to figure out how to help an AI company maintain a large lead or spend that lead wisely. Instead, we need to figure out how to help them upgrade their risk management culture.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fntvlyewznjdb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftvlyewznjdb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We could imagine extreme scenarios where state-of-the-art safety work is (i) useless, or (ii) self-executing. If safety work is useless, then what it means to “implement” those techniques is mostly just noticing that frontier AI is unsafe and coordinating efforts to make sure nobody trains or deploys new models until that changes. If safety work is so powerful as to be self-executing, i.e., the mere discovery of the technique causes the technique to be implemented, then what it means to “implement” those techniques is mostly just discovering them in time. I’m skeptical of both extremes, because I think that it’s much more likely that the value of safety techniques will be heavily influenced by how quickly, thoroughly, and competently they are adopted by frontier AI developers.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsif28pc5yv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsif28pc5yv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The most relevant decision-makers are probably the executives at frontier AI developers, but if you think that a government or an embedded evaluator will be making the most important decisions, then the scale can be used to measure the risk management culture inside the government or the evaluator.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7Qe2wP9Hi4Eq4QacA/argue-about-risk-management-speed-not-p-doom#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Humans can (almost) natively read neural network weights](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uvKSq6eWkud997ie7/humans-can-almost-natively-read-neural-network-weights)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://rikiyat.github.io/blog/weight-space-programming/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Takehi et al.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> report that GPT-6-Astra can intuit the functions of neural networks by performing static analysis of the weights. I argue that, for at least two of these experiments, this is much more a feat of numerical gestalt than of mechanistic understanding. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the first experiment, Astra is given the weights of 4 identically-sized neural networks, each trained to play a different basic video game. It is asked to identify, without running the network or using any tools, the game being played, as well as make some observations about the network itself, how the agent in the game gains reward, etc. The prompt for this task explicitly names the 5 input channels as agent, moving objects, reward targets, obstacles, and previous frame. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I agree that, given the literal task as described above, a human would not be able to identify the game from the raw floats. But humans are inherently terrible at parsing long lists of numbers; we invented a discipline called Data Visualization long ago to address this shortcoming. So, if we allow the absolute most basic Dataviz trick we can think of - averaging the first-layer weights across each channel and plotting them on a heatmap - the task suddenly becomes </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">much</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> easier, arguably trivial for anyone with at least some familiarity with the sorts of games often used to train toy neural networks. Try and guess:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Anonymous game Network A: agent, moving objects, reward targets and previous-frame weight maps." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793765067/lexical_client_uploads/r4fbg7xmflqiacrzlzzk.png" /></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="Anonymous game Network B: agent, moving objects, reward targets and previous-frame weight maps." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793765068/lexical_client_uploads/xvx3xosd2biidfjhfw2q.png" /></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="Anonymous game Network C: agent, moving objects, reward targets and previous-frame weight maps." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793765068/lexical_client_uploads/mdygsoc25fbrwilqqjzs.png" /></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="Anonymous game Network D: agent, moving objects, reward targets and previous-frame weight maps." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793765068/lexical_client_uploads/mswl44r5yvttqag3rlcg.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I haven't included the obstacle channel, because under the naive normalisation it sort of just looks like noise. Principal component analysis recovers an outline of the game board in each case, but that's straying a bit far from the already slightly hyperbolic title of this post, and besides, it's unnecessary. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The gif below serves both to reveal the answers and to illustrate how trivially 'interpretable' these networks are. </span></p><img alt="all_games_reveal.gif" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793764769/lexical_client_uploads/o3u3xkec9hh3rxvhghqj.gif" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For the second experiment, Astra is given a denoising diffusion model that produces 12x12px grayscale images, and some information about the size and shape of the network. Under the same setup (weights only, no tools) it is asked to figure out what images each network generates. In this case, simply averaging the final layer's weights and plotting them on a 12x12 grid gets you this:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Three anonymous diffusion models, each shown as the mean absolute output weight at every pixel." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793763179/lexical_client_uploads/fpxb1ap1acmmmknccg9h.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm not going to bother with a gif reveal for this one; show it to a five-year-old if you're struggling (or try squinting a bit). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So yes, it's cool that Astra can read a string of 17,000 or so floating point numbers and do something akin to plotting a heatmap in its head. But I don't think it proves anything particularly impressive or unexpected about LLM capabilities. Models have long since been superhuman at specific things like 'reading thousands of floating point characters and answering questions about them', because humans are really, really bad at that sort of thing.  </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After drafting this, I was pointed to </span><a href="https://www.seangoedecke.com/llms-are-not-superhuman-at-reading-and-writing-neural-network-weights/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sean Goedecke’s post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which makes much the same argument about the game networks. He also discusses how the headline gain on Qwen3.5-0.8b's math performance results from simply deleting a looping direction, and that this used standard mechanistic interpretability tools to identify the direction responsible. The prompt for that task even includes the phrase "for example, if the model often fails because it repeats itself and never finishes, finding and fixing what drives that repetition counts". Fixing this repetition turns out to be responsible for essentially all of the model's performance gain. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While I find the general research direction interesting, I believe the results in this paper are presented in such a way as to give a misleading view of frontier model's capabilities concerning mechanistic interpretability and invoke the fabled, dreaded specter of Recursive Self Improvement. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Data: Rikiya Takehi, Jayden Teoh, Alex Zhang and Omar Khattab, </span><a href="https://github.com/RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">released weights and logs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CC BY 4.0</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Figures are </span><a href="https://github.com/crabbixOCE/weight-space-figures" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">original visualizations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of their released weights, created by GPT-6.1-sol.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uvKSq6eWkud997ie7/humans-can-almost-natively-read-neural-network-weights#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [What are the concrete arguments for "AI pause would significantly hurt the economy."](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aXA73PnhdJXPb4SWn/what-are-the-concrete-arguments-for-ai-pause-would)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've seen a bunch of people claiming "well, we need to make sure whatever our AI policy is doesn't hurt the economy because people will feel that in their wallets and hate it (and hurting the economy is bad."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This might hypothetically be true, but I've never seen anyone argue for it in any detail.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Meanwhile, I'd naively expect if shut down AI anytime soon, it'd dampen growth, and maybe it'd be a minor economic dip, but I wouldn't expect it to be a major recession. (It makes sense that it'd become a bigger deal as AI takes over more of the economy. But it feels like the people saying "it'd hurt the economy" are often the people implying that means we should do less of a pause and do it later).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't know much about economic recessions. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But, like, for reference, here's the 2007-2009 recession and the Covid blip.</span></p><p><b><strong>GDP</strong></b></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793914075/lexical_client_uploads/mx3jkejo0ovkcnewmxju.png" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://fred.stlouisfed.org/series/GDP"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://fred.stlouisfed.org/series/GDP</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><b><strong>Unemployment</strong></b></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793914202/lexical_client_uploads/bw7kdoozxpgpybzupgsr.png" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://www.bls.gov/charts/employment-situation/civilian-unemployment-rate.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://www.bls.gov/charts/employment-situation/civilian-unemployment-rate.htm</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I asked Claude</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the frontier AI industry and datacenter build out completely halted, what do you predict would happen to US unemployment and GDP?</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude said:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><strong>My central estimate: a sudden, complete halt would likely cause a US recession. Unemployment would probably peak around 5.5–6.5%, up from roughly 4.2–4.4% today. Real GDP would end up about 2–4% below where it would otherwise be within 12–18 months.</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> That would be worse than the 2001 dot-com bust but well short of 2008.</span></p></blockquote><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full response</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Starting point</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><b><strong>Labor market:</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> September’s report showed unemployment at 4.2%, and the rate has stayed in a narrow 4.1%–4.3% band since March 2026. Hiring is already weak, with payroll gains in the tens of thousands. The October report comes out tomorrow. </span><a href="https://www.insiderfinance.io/news/u-s-jobs-report-undershoots-forecasts" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">insiderfinance</span></a></li><li value="2"><b><strong>Growth:</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Real GDP grew at a 3.4% annualized rate in Q3 2026. Information processing equipment and software contributed 1.09 points of that, about 32%. Private investment in data-center structures rose roughly 69% year over year, to an $83 billion annual rate. </span><a href="https://fourweekmba.com/ai-us-data-center-spending-hits-record-83b-pace-gdp-up-3-4/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fourweekmbafourweekmba</span></a></li><li value="3"><b><strong>Scale of spending:</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Amazon, Alphabet, Meta, Microsoft and SpaceX are on pace to spend $803–828 billion on capex in 2026, and executives say they will spend more in 2027. Estimates of AI’s share of growth vary by method. ING’s James Knightley puts AI investment at roughly a third of US growth this year. Bridgewater estimates AI capex adds about 1.4 points to full-year 2026 growth. </span><a href="https://www.hl.co.uk/news/ai-hyperscalers-are-expected-to-finish-the-year-spending-a-whopping-828-billion-on-capex" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI hyperscalers are expected to finish the year spending a whopping $828 billion on capex +2</span></a></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Channels of the shock</span></h3><p><b><strong>1. Direct investment loss, worth about 1.5–2.5% of GDP.</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Gross AI capex is over $1 trillion when you add Oracle, the neoclouds and the labs’ own builds, against a GDP of roughly $31 trillion. But much of that money never counted as US output. Chips come from TSMC, servers are assembled in Mexico and Taiwan, and memory comes from Korea. Accounting studies find the net effect on US GDP is substantially smaller than the raw investment figures suggest once imports are subtracted. That leakage is the biggest cushion: Taiwan and Korea would absorb a large share of the hit. </span><a href="https://api.emergentmind.com/papers/2601.11196" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">emergentmind</span></a></p><p><b><strong>2. Wealth effect, worth about 1–1.5% of GDP.</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AI-linked firms make up a large part of US equity market value. A halt would likely cut stock prices by 25–40%, erasing perhaps $10–15 trillion of household wealth. At the usual estimate of 2–4 cents of lost spending per dollar of lost wealth, consumption would fall about 1–1.5% of GDP. High-income households, who own most of the stock, now drive a large share of consumer spending.</span></p><p><b><strong>3. Credit stress.</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Bank of America expects hyperscaler debt issuance to reach $175 billion in 2026, more than six times the prior five-year average. Data-center debt, private credit and GPU-backed loans would see losses. Neoclouds and developers would default first, and lending to other borrowers could tighten. </span><a href="https://officechai.com/ai/ai-capex-spend-at-top-4-hyperscalers-to-touch-715-billion-in-2026/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">officechai</span></a></p><p><b><strong>4. Jobs.</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The direct job loss is modest relative to the GDP hit, because the AI build-out is capital-intensive rather than labor-intensive. I’d estimate 0.5–1 million jobs in data-center construction, electrical trades, power generation, chip and equipment manufacturing, and AI firms themselves. The second-round effects would be larger: lower consumer spending, tighter credit and weaker business confidence. By the usual rule of thumb (Okun’s law), a 2–4% output gap implies unemployment roughly 1–2 points higher.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Offsets</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><b><strong>The Fed has room to cut,</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and lower rates would help housing and other rate-sensitive sectors, though only after a lag.</span></li><li value="2"><b><strong>Skilled trades would find other work.</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Electricians and grid workers are in short supply elsewhere, so they would be absorbed faster than, say, 2009’s construction workers.</span></li><li value="3"><b><strong>Energy prices would ease.</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Electricity and natural-gas demand would drop, which helps households and manufacturers.</span></li><li value="4"><b><strong>Some AI-related layoffs might stop.</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If AI is currently displacing white-collar workers, a halt would remove that drag. I think this effect is small relative to the demand shock.</span></li><li value="5"><b><strong>Fiscal policy would probably respond.</strong></b></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scenarios</span></h3><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><br /></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Peak unemployment</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDP vs. baseline (12–18 mo)</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Benign (fast Fed cuts, orderly market, high import leakage)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">~5%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">−1 to −2%</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Central</span></p></td><td class="table-cell" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">~5.5–6.5%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">−2 to −4%</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Severe (credit spiral, 40%+ equity crash, weak policy response)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">7%+</span></p></td><td class="table-cell" style="background-color: initial; border: 1px solid black; width: 75px; vertical-align: top; text-align: start;"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">−4 to −6%</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The closest historical comparison is 2001, when an IT-investment bust pushed unemployment from about 4% to 6.3% even though GDP barely fell. Two differences make today’s case worse: the halt you describe is abrupt rather than gradual, and AI investment is a larger share of growth now than telecom and IT were then. On the other hand, more of the spending leaks abroad.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Caveats</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are judgment calls, not model output. The biggest uncertainty is how far equity prices fall and whether credit losses spread beyond AI firms. The scenario also assumes nothing replaces the spending. In reality, some of the capital would go into other investments over time, so the long-run loss is mostly the forgone productivity gains from AI rather than permanently lost demand.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sources:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://fourweekmba.com/ai-us-data-center-spending-hits-record-83b-pace-gdp-up-3-4/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">US Data Center Spending Hits Record $83B Pace; GDP Up 3.4% — FourWeekMBA (citing BEA)</span></a></li><li value="2"><a href="https://think.ing.com/opinions/how-much-is-ai-contributing-to-us-economic-growth/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How much is AI contributing to US economic growth? — ING THINK</span></a></li><li value="3"><a href="https://fourweekmba.com/ai-ai-gdp-load-bearing-us-economy-bloomberg-morgan-stanley-brid/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Is Now Load-Bearing for the US Economy — FourWeekMBA</span></a></li><li value="4"><a href="https://www.hl.co.uk/news/ai-hyperscalers-are-expected-to-finish-the-year-spending-a-whopping-828-billion-on-capex"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI hyperscalers expected to spend $828 billion on capex — Hargreaves Lansdown</span></a></li><li value="5"><a href="https://officechai.com/ai/ai-capex-spend-at-top-4-hyperscalers-to-touch-715-billion-in-2026/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Capex Spend at Top 4 Hyperscalers — OfficeChai</span></a></li><li value="6"><a href="https://www.insiderfinance.io/news/u-s-jobs-report-undershoots-forecasts"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">U.S. Jobs Report Undershoots Forecasts — InsiderFinance</span></a></li><li value="7"><a href="https://api.emergentmind.com/papers/2601.11196"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI and the US Economy: An Accounting Perspective — EmergentMind summary</span></a></li></ul><p><br /></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I asked DeepSeek, who I go to for more decorrelated answers. (I added the second paragraph because it answers very quickly and vaguely the first time)</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the frontier AI industry and datacenter build out completely halted, what do you predict would happen to US unemployment and GDP?</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Think through all the considerations you can think of, and then give quantitative answer with an upper and lower bound of percentage points for both</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deepseek said:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If frontier AI and datacenter buildout halted completely, the most defensible quantitative estimate is: </span><b><strong>GDP growth would fall by roughly 0.4 to 1.0 percentage point, and the unemployment rate would rise by roughly 0.2 to 0.6 percentage points</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, relative to a baseline where the buildout continues.</span></p></blockquote><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full response</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If frontier AI and datacenter buildout halted completely, the most defensible quantitative estimate is: </span><b><strong>GDP growth would fall by roughly 0.4 to 1.0 percentage point, and the unemployment rate would rise by roughly 0.2 to 0.6 percentage points</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, relative to a baseline where the buildout continues.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These ranges are not symmetrical in confidence. The GDP impact is better grounded in available estimates; the unemployment impact is more uncertain because data-center construction employment is diffuse and hard to isolate from broader construction and tech hiring.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDP Impact: 0.4 to 1.0 Percentage Point Hit to Growth</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The key distinction is between </span><b><strong>gross AI-related spending</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and its </span><b><strong>net contribution to GDP after imports</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Much of the hardware—chips, servers, networking equipment—is imported, so a halt would also reduce imports, partially offsetting the GDP drag.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><strong>Net contribution estimate</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: One analysis found that AI-related spending contributed about </span><b><strong>0.4 to 0.5 percentage points</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to real GDP growth in the first three quarters of 2025 on a net basis, after accounting for imports . Removing that entirely implies a drag of roughly that magnitude.</span></li><li value="2"><b><strong>Gross investment scale</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: A Brookings estimate projected datacenter and AI infrastructure investment at an average of </span><b><strong>3.6% of GDP annually</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from 2025 to 2032 . A sudden halt would remove a large planned investment flow, but because GDP is a flow measure and the investment is spread over years, the annual growth impact is smaller than the stock figure suggests.</span></li><li value="3"><b><strong>CBO’s productivity view</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: The Congressional Budget Office estimated AI-driven productivity gains would add only about </span><b><strong>10 basis points per year</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to nominal GDP, suggesting the productivity channel is small in the near term .</span></li></ul><p><b><strong>Lower bound (0.4 pp)</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Assumes imports offset a large share of the investment decline and that non-AI consumption remains resilient, as MRB Partners argued . </span><b><strong>Upper bound (1.0 pp)</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Assumes broader multiplier effects—construction payrolls, supplier demand, and local spending—amplify the direct investment loss.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unemployment Impact: 0.2 to 0.6 Percentage Point Rise</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The employment channel is real but smaller than the headline “millions of jobs” framing suggests. The datacenter industry’s claimed </span><b><strong>4.7 million jobs</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in 2023 is a broad economic-footprint figure, not a count of workers who would be laid off if buildout stopped. The directly exposed workforce is narrower.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><strong>Construction concentration</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Datacenter construction spending rose </span><b><strong>64.5%</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> through August 2026 while most other private construction categories contracted . This means a halt would hit a sector that has been a rare source of blue-collar job strength.</span></li><li value="2"><b><strong>Permanent operations roles</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Projected permanent datacenter positions were around </span><b><strong>650,000 by 2026</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, with </span><b><strong>340,000 unfilled</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> . A halt to new buildout would freeze rather than eliminate existing operations jobs, but it would stop hiring and likely trigger layoffs in construction and equipment installation.</span></li><li value="3"><b><strong>Baseline context</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: The unemployment rate was </span><b><strong>4.2%</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in September 2026 . A 0.2–0.6 pp rise would lift it to roughly </span><b><strong>4.4–4.8%</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—a meaningful softening but not a recession-level shock on its own.</span></li></ul><p><b><strong>Lower bound (0.2 pp)</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Assumes most construction workers transition to other projects and the permanent operations workforce is largely retained. </span><b><strong>Upper bound (0.6 pp)</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Assumes construction layoffs are concentrated and slow to absorb, plus knock-on losses in supplier and local service employment.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What This Estimate Leaves Out</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This forecast does </span><b><strong>not</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> include a financial-market or confidence channel. If a halt were abrupt and perceived as a signal about tech-sector weakness, equity repricing and tighter financial conditions could amplify the GDP and unemployment effects beyond the ranges above. Those second-order effects are genuinely unquantifiable from available data and would push the upper bounds higher, not lower.</span></p><p><b><strong>Bottom line</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: The AI buildout is a meaningful but not dominant near-term driver of US growth and employment. A complete halt would be a noticeable drag—roughly half a point of GDP growth and a few tenths on unemployment—but it would not by itself push the US into recession.</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These both seem pretty weaksauce, both in terms of "how bad in absolute terms" and "how much people would get angry by feeling it in their wallet and making things politically more difficult." I'd expect the economy would recover pretty quickly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As far as I can tell the economic fears are... just made up? Or, blown way out of proportion? Yes, there will be nonzero tradeoffs. Okay, but, this does not seem remotely commensurate with "superintelligence is carelessly built and deployed" even if you're way more optimistic about superintelligence than me.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again, I'd expect more economic turmoil the later you pause and the more the economy has gotten bundled up around AI. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(And, of course, the object level badness of AI rushing ahead is crazy compared to this. And if you care about the upside of AI, the choices are still not "never AI ever" vs "let a rip", it's "slow down and do it more carefully and more reliably reap all the benefits" vs "gamble that we get the benefits a few years or maybe decades earlier but risk killing or disempowering everyone")</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We'd also very unlikely to end up shutting down the industry completely. The datacenter buildout would get repurposed for inference. Anthropic and OpenAI would become "normal-ish product companies." Some of their investors may be screwed, but, like, that's their problem?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does anyone have good arguments that either it's going to be worse than Claude/Deepseek guess, or that I should take the amount-of-bad they guess more seriously?</span></p><p><i><em>[edit: after thinking about it more, I do take "minor recession" more seriously. It'd be both a real harm and a real political-capital risk. </em></i></p><p><i><em>But, still feels like the way I hear people talk seems like they think it more inevitable than seems obvious, in any regime that is merely a 'slowdown' vs sudden, total pause. And, talking about it with a missing mood.]</em></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aXA73PnhdJXPb4SWn/what-are-the-concrete-arguments-for-ai-pause-would#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Where's all the blackmail?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bJtBvxMcbDSNdzveA/where-s-all-the-blackmail)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is interesting how little blackmail I see, that I know is blackmail. A lot of people probably do some things they would be embarrassed by if they were made public at some degree of detail and explicitness, and it probably isn’t that hard to get evidence of these, and quite a few people seem to be amoral . Also, in order to properly embarrass someone, I imagine you would make your information relatively public, so that for instance I—Katja—should hear about some fraction of cases. Yet I very rarely receive information I know to be public for the purpose of embarrassing someone. I’m not sure I remember it ever happening. Why not?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I imagine being morally inclined toward blackmailing someone, one salient reason to avoid it is that my wrongdoing is extremely obvious and in-the-face of the recipient, so they seem likely to react destructively, for instance by telling everyone that I’m a dirty blackmailer. It’s like stealing from right in front of someone’s face.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another possibility is that there is a lot of blackmail, but it mostly remains private because the blackmailers mostly get the price right to have their threats heeded. This is an interesting theory, because it suggests blackmail is much more common than we observe, so people may be greatly underestimating the chance of it happening to them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One place I have heard about blackmail occurring is from AI systems. Which is interesting, if it isn’t a thing humans seem to be doing much. What to make of that?</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bJtBvxMcbDSNdzveA/where-s-all-the-blackmail#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The Data Product Platform Navigator](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Many organizations adopting data mesh and data products face a common
     challenge. They may be tasked with building a self-service platform for
     data products and data mesh from scratch, or they may have an existing
     platform that suffers from low adoption, preventing their data mesh from
     scaling. This article provides a platform navigator, based on the CNCF's
     Platform Engineering Maturity Model, to help you reason about your current
     situation and determine the next steps for your platform.</p>
